TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* Against an Axis of Anything
  Foreign Policy | 26 Nov 00:36 UTC
  China and Russia are getting closer, but their alignment is hardly inevitable.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Those picking up a copy of the Economist this week might have been struck with an unpleasant sense of nostalgia. Readers were greeted with a front cover emblazoned in red and black, with a screaming headline proclaiming the “ axis of malice .” Reminiscent of the war on terror and all its fearmongering excesses, this headline was widely mocked on social media as a reminder of the George W. Bush era. Those who mocked it were in turn derided as naive ; growing connections among Russia, China, Iran, and North Korea surely, others argued, prove the existence of such an axis. Those picking up a copy of the Economist this week might have been struck with an unpleasant sense of nostalgia. Readers were greeted with a front cover emblazoned in red and black, with a screaming headline proclaiming the “ axis of malice .” Reminiscent of the war on terror and all its fearmongering excesses, this headline was widely mocked on social media as a reminder of the George W. Bush era. Those who mocked it were in turn derided as naive ; growing connections among Russia, China, Iran, and North Korea surely, others argued, prove the existence of such an axis. But most of this debate missed the point. No one—or at least very few people—would deny that we have seen ties between China and Russia grow in recent years. The use of the axis framing, however, and of language like “malice,” creates a narrative that does not necessarily exist in practice. It suggests that the only response to growing alignment between these states is adversarial, inhibits us from any real debate about the extent and scope of this coordination, and perhaps most importantly, suggests that this process of alignment is inevitable, rather than something that can be molded or changed. Such overly simplistic and Manichaean visions weaken, rather than strengthen, our ability to understand the world. None of this is new. The “ axis of evil ” first emerged as a term in President George W. Bush’s 2002 State of the Union address, where he referred to Iran, Iraq, and North Korea as countries that endangered freedom around the world. Even at the time, the inclusion of the line in the speech was somewhat strange: It took place just a few months after the Sept. 11 attacks, and shortly after the United States had invaded Afghanistan, yet the three countries representing “evil” had nothing to do with either. But the overblown rhetoric helped to build support for Bush’s Freedom Agen
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/25/against-axis-of-anything/

The Trump administration on Wednesday proposed a fresh batch of tariffs on more than a dozen major trading partners, including China, Japan, South Korea, and the European Union. The proposed tariff rates, which could be implemented early next year, range from 15 percent for the EU, Taiwan, and South Korea, 18 percent for most others, and rates of as much as 25 percent on India or 35 percent on Vietnam. China faces a fresh tariff rate of 20 percent, which will be stacked on top of duties still lingering from U.S. President Donald Trump’s first term—and that’s only if the trade truce between Beijing and Washington holds. (It expires in January.) The latest tariffs are the culmination of an investigation launched this spring into alleged excess industrial capacity among nearly all U.S. trading partners. U.S. trade officials allege that other countries artificially prop up their manufacturing sectors with subsidies, promotions, tax breaks, and other measures that distort trade and disadvantage U.S. exports. U.S. President Donald Trump, meanwhile, has taken equity stakes in dozens of private companies and is actively promoting state involvement in the manufacture of everything from chips to ships. China’s Foreign Ministry said the new tariffs are based on a “false proposition,” though China is one of the few countries among the 14 economies targeted that demonstrates genuine excess capacity that is harming the U.S. and global economies. Even trade specialists who are skeptical of the Trump administration’s affinity for tariffs believe that higher duties on Chinese exports are warranted. The European Commission also hit back at the tariffs, arguing that Europe is a victim of overcapacity, not a source of the problem. The Trump administration is using Section 301 of the 1974 Trade A

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In the small eastern German city of Dessau, if the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party has its way, a century-old vision for the future may soon belong to the past. A short walk away from the main train station, past typical German architecture of beige stucco and pitched, red-tiled roofs, flat-roofed, modernist structures of concrete and glass appear, leading to a building with “BAUHAUS” spelled down one gray side. Designed by pioneering modernist architect Walter Gropius, the building in Dessau opened as a school for architecture, craft, and industrial design on Dec. 4, 1926—one with ambitions to create a new aesthetic for the modern world. With its steel-framed windows and a bright red entrance door, the Bauhaus school building is an iconic symbol of early 20th-century modernism. In the small eastern German city of Dessau, if the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party has its way, a century-old vision for the future may soon belong to the past. A short walk away from the main train station, past typical German architecture of beige stucco and pitched, red-tiled roofs, flat-roofed, modernist structures of concrete and glass appear, leading to a building with “BAUHAUS” spelled down one gray side. Designed by pioneering modernist architect Walter Gropius, the building in Dessau opened as a school for architecture, craft, and industrial design on Dec. 4, 1926—one with ambitions to create a new aesthetic for the modern world. With its steel-framed windows and a bright red entrance door, the Bauhaus school building is an iconic symbol of early 20th-century modernism. It’s also a symbol of Nazi persecution. In 1932, the school closed under pressure from state Nazi officials that denounced the Bauhaus as un-German, internationalist, and a hotbed of “cultural Bolshevism.” (It briefly operated in Berlin, until it fell to Nazi pressure there, too.) Many of its members fled the Nazi regime and are now considered some of the last century’s most influential luminaries of art, architecture, and design. Nazis burn anti-German literature in Berlin on May 11, 1933. Clear, simple, and functional, Bauhaus’s influence has been a key German cultural export for more than a century. Yet Hans-Thomas Tillschneider, a culture policy spokesman for the AfD, considers it an “ Irrweg der Moderne ”—“a wrong turn of modernity.” For years, he has targeted the Bauhaus, arguing that it stands for “deracination” and “dislocation o
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/25/germany-afd-culture-policy-bauhaus/

* De Gaulle and the Art of European Defiance
  Foreign Policy | 25 Nov 20:00 UTC
  One of the year’s best films carries a potent message for our Trumpian age.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. With year-end best-of lists about to arrive, one strong contender for film of the year might seem unlikely at first: a five-hour, two-part, largely French-language biopic of French general and statesman Charles de Gaulle that only recently reached U.S. screens. Directed by Antonin Baudry and starring a strikingly de Gaulle-like Simon Abkarian, this French blockbuster comes with an almost-American budget of 74 million euros, one of the largest in the history of francophone cinema. Perhaps unsurprisingly given its subject, it became the hit of the French summer, offering audiences a nostalgic vision of national greatness at a moment of paralysis and division. More surprisingly, the de Gaulle diptych has won praise outside of France as well, helped by the fact that it works superbly as both action and entertainment. Epic in scale, beautifully shot, both wryly amusing and often touching, its twin installments pass far more quickly than their daunting runtime might suggest. There are clear contemporary echoes, too, in de Gaulle’s personal struggles and story of dogged European defiance. Indeed, it is not hard to see why viewers in France and elsewhere have warmed to this tale of an embattled European leader fighting to re-build sovereignty in the face of pressure from the United States as the moorings of a crumbling old order give way to something frightening and new. Progress has been slow, and Nepal remains one of the only South Asian countries today with no BRI projects on its soil. However, Nepal, like other smaller South Asian states, relies heavily on Indian development and humanitarian assistance.   FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The recent summit between U.S. President Donald Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese President Xi Jinping promised to “conduct a dialogue on reducing nuclear risks and preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons.” But what that means is unclear, especially when arms control itself has become a politically fraught term across multiple spheres. The expiration of New START, a 2011 treaty that built on past arms control efforts, in February left the United States and Russia without legally binding limits on their deployed strategic nuclear forces. Some North Korea watchers worry that arms control negotiations would mean abandoning denuclearization as a policy objective, even though interim measures could contribute to the longer-term goal. Meanwhile, China has long resisted trilateral arms control talks, citing the disparity between its arsenal and those of the United States and Russia. The Shenzen trilateral declaration nevertheless represents a modest but potentially meaningful diplomatic opening. China’s willingness to discuss nuclear dangers is noteworthy given its resistance to negotiations on nuclear arms limitations. However, the declaration provides no timetable, no delegation, and no agreement on specific measures. It remains an open question whether the three countries can translate that commitment into practical cooperation. Arms control does not have to begin with a treaty. * Media reaction: Who is to blame for COP31 ‘failure’?
  Carbon Brief | 25 Nov 16:09 UTC
  COP31 came to a dramatic close last weekend when a coalition of nations rejected the proposed “Antalya pact”, citing its… The post Media reaction: Who is to blame for COP31 ‘failure’? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: COP31 came to a dramatic close last weekend when a coalition of nations rejected the proposed “Antalya pact”, citing its failure to mention fossil fuels. The tense final moments and lack of a much-anticipated “pact” have sparked extensive media coverage around the world. Many commentators have rushed to label the COP31 outcome a “ failure ”, questioning what it means for the future of the UN climate process and a “ divided world ”. There has been much finger-pointing, with some outlets attributing COP31’s “collapse” to “ oil-rich nations ”, while others blame Europe’s “ reluctance ” to provide more climate finance. While it is not accurate to say there was “ no agreement ” at COP31, the media response demonstrates the complexity of the divergent views on display at the Antalya conference. In this article, Carbon Brief draws together media reactions from around the world to show the diversity of responses to the COP31 outcome. COP31 ended without agreement on a proposed “Antalya pact”, which had been pushed by the Turkey-Australia presidency. The final text was rejected by at least 32 countries, including the EU, the UK and some climate-vulnerable countries, in chaotic scenes as the talks overran by two days. An Irish Times editorial said the outcome was an “unprecedented ending of a COP without any agreement or roadmap forward”. This view, while commonly expressed in the media, is incorrect. As veteran “COP-watchers” explained for an article on CB Connect , the summit reached agreement on many decisions, even though the presidency’s “cover decision” failed. Referring to the rejection of the cover decision, Melbourne University climate researchers Prof Jacqueline Peel and Prof Kathryn Bowen wrote in the Conversation that “these texts have always been add-ons to the formal negotiation process”. They noted that there was “progress in a few areas” at COP31, including negotiations on supporting a just transition via a “phased approach” and “improving alignment in climate adaptation”. For a full overview of what happened at COP31 – both inside and outside the negotiations – see Carbon Brief ’s “key outcomes” article. In the aftermath of COP31, commentators have critiqued decisions made by the presidency. An editorial in the Hindu noted that “usually, cover texts evolve as a final synthesis of deliberations”, but said the COP31 leadership “thought it fit to push for a cover text early in the proceedings”. Australian “president of negotiations” Chris Bowen has als
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/media-reaction-who-is-to-blame-for-cop31-failure

The post The Unraveling Promise of Berlin in Ahmad Abdalla’s ‘BerleeN_’ appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Over the last decade and a half, Berlin came to represent the possibility of a new beginning for a generation of Arabs reeling from the revolutions of the Arab Spring. The German capital became a magnet for refugees, dissidents, writers, intellectuals, artists and political exiles from Syria, Egypt, Iraq, Sudan and other parts of the region, who were looking for the freedoms that had eluded them back home. The city’s famous counterculture, growing Arab creative scenes, relatively low cost of living and geographic proximity to the region helped establish its reputation as a sort of postrevolutionary sanctuary. Yet that image seems to be increasingly difficult to sustain. Germany’s crackdown on Palestinian solidarity movements and growing hostility toward migrants, fueled by the rise of the far right, has exposed the limits of this reputed space of political and artistic freedom. This ever more unsettling landscape is where renowned Egyptian filmmaker Ahmad Abdalla places his latest film, “BerleeN_” (the Arabic pronunciation of the city’s name). The surreal feature-length drama just saw its world premiere at this year’s edition of the Cairo International Film Festival and won the Youssef Cherif Rizkallah Audience Award (Abdalla also received the Next Generation Award in the Established Talent category). Yosra El Lozy plays May, a Syrian food influencer being pursued by a mysterious old man who, we later learn, has mistaken her for a woman from Aleppo. Her visit to another Syrian friend who never leaves his apartment offers a subtler glimpse of displacement, and of the different ways its psychological toll can manifest. Developing these plots required partnering with other owners and covering construction costs. Although residents were also entitled to alternative housing or rental allowances, many say they received neither adequate compensation nor the housing they were promised. Rules requiring continuous occupancy meant that residents who had fled abroad and were absent during official inspections lost their entitlement to alternative housing. People suspected of terrorism, even without formal charges, were also reportedly excluded. * Is mental illness universal?
  Aeon | 24 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Do all cultures experience the same mental disorders? Or are our experiences of mental distress culturally specific? - by Alexander Pereira Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Subway station in People’s Square, Shanghai, China, 2011. Photo by Carlos Barria/Reuters On a November day in 1994, a 14-year-old girl in Hong Kong named Charlene Hsu Chi-Ying collapsed on a busy sidewalk, in broad daylight, in her school uniform. She had starved herself to death. Charlene had never been diagnosed with anorexia, not because her self-starvation went unnoticed, but because the diagnosis, in its Western form, was mostly unknown in the East. Her death changed that overnight. Splashed across the front pages were stark headlines such as ‘Girl Who Died In Street Was A Walking Skeleton’. Local journalists scrambled for an explanation of this tragedy, and quickly found a template ready-made: a Western account of what anorexia was, what it felt like, who got it, what caused it. The ideas spread rapidly – particularly that anorexia involved distorted self-perceptions about one’s body (dysmorphia) and an intense, borderline delusional, fear of gaining weight, often cued by prevailing beauty standards (notably, a cultural fixation with slender female bodies). Mass public education campaigns followed. Hotlines opened, celebrities spoke out. Anorexia became part of the zeitgeist. Before Charlene’s death, the psychiatrist Sing Lee had been quietly studying a small number of women in Hong Kong who were starving themselves to dangerous degrees. Most of these women did not obsess over their bodies, nor fear becoming fat. They instead complained of somatic (bodily) ailments: uncomfortable bloating, a profound lack of appetite. The cognitive hallmark of Western anorexia – a distorted body image – was largely absent. Lee seemed to be documenting a distinct form of self-starvation with its own local logic. The situation has changed dramatically since then. By 2007, more than three-quarters of the anorexic patients Lee treated cited ‘fat phobia’ as a central motivation. Today, anorexia in Hong Kong is terrifyingly common, affecting roughly one in 200 schoolgirls – comparable to trends in the United States. We do not know the form that Charlene’s illness took. What we do know is that her death became a catalyst for the rapid importation of a Western conceptual framework that reshaped how Hong Kong understood and experienced disordered eating in young women. Anorexia is just one example from a compendium of curious cases in which culture seemingly shapes mental disorders. For example, anthropologists have documented how cultures sculpt ‘idioms of distress’ – social
  https://aeon.co/essays/are-mental-illnesses-universal-or-are-they-culturally-specific

* The Influencers’ Influencer
  Longreads | 24 Nov 10:00 UTC
  MFK Fisher’s clandestine campaign to spread Japanese cuisine and transform American food culture
  FULL TEXT: This essay is co-published with Vittles and appears in Vittles Issue No. 3: The Influencers . Vaughn Tan | Longreads | November 24, 2026 | 3,989 words (17 minutes) Back in 2003, I was in the early stages of researching how the distinctive visual style of Japanese plating and dish design influenced plating outside Japan, and wanted to analyze photos of fine-dining dishes in industry periodicals like Art Culinaire . Back in the early 2000s, this was easiest to do in libraries. At the time, I lived in Cambridge, Massachusetts, so I wound up in the collection of historical cookbooks and culinary materials at the Schlesinger Library. One afternoon, Barbara Haber, then the Schlesinger’s curator of books, stopped by my desk with something for me: Japanese Cooking: A Simple Art by Shizuo Tsuji, published in 1980 with an introduction by MFK Fisher. I knew of Fisher through her essays on eating and her translation of Brillat-Savarin’s The Physiology of Taste , but not for anything related to Japanese food. Barbara mentioned that many boxes of Fisher’s collected papers had recently arrived at the library. The papers were still uncatalogued, but was I interested in looking at them? I didn’t know then that I was beginning a 20-year detour. As I went through these boxes over the next few months, my original research project faded away. I found myself more and more drawn to the Fisher archive boxes, and to the story that emerged as I read through years of her correspondence and other documents, including letters about her trip to Japan to research the introduction she wrote to Japanese Cooking ; letters about the book she wrote to journalists, writers, and editors across the US; and draft manuscripts of Japanese Cooking , with her comments in red pen, which had crisscrossed the Pacific between California and Osaka. The story that emerged was of a secret campaign Fisher waged between 1979 and 1982, which was instrumental in the seemingly sudden transformation of Japanese food into fine dining in America. The Fisher I uncovered in those boxes was someone else, and her role in making Japanese food into fine dining in America remains almost entirely unknown. Fisher’s campaign worked well because it was largely invisible. The publishers of Japanese Cooking didn’t know that any of this was happening. Ruth Reichl, who wrote the foreword to the 25th anniversary edition in 2005, had no idea Fisher had done any more than write the introduction when I spoke to her earlier this year
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/24/mfk-fisher-influencer-japanese-food-america/

That dynamic is reflected in the relationship between the nations’ two intelligence agencies. “ The CIA is a colossal organization, whereas Bond, however ingenious he might be, is just a bloke with some sexual troubles and a gun,” Winder says. In many ways, Bond became the antithesis of the real politicians who represented Britain on the world stage. In the Suez Crisis, for example, “you can see what Anthony Eden is: ill, old, bust up, nostalgic for a previous period, wishes people in the Middle East just did as they were told,” Winder notes. “And there’s Nasser, who represents a particular form of virile, tough, non-class-based authority. … What do you do in the face of such a wave of anti-colonial energy, which Nasser fronted?” For Winder, Britain’s declining influence has meant the franchise has lost much of its relevance. “The fact he’s British, I think, with every passing year gets more and more stupid, because there’s quite a lot of countries where he’d be lucky to get a visa. Britain’s place in the world is really very local,” he says. Nonetheless, Winder approves of the announcement of Robert Pattinson as the seventh Bond in the franchise’s history. * The catch in IBM’s offer to relocate its U.S.-based immigrant workers
  Rest of World | 23 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Ending U.S. visa sponsorship and shifting roles abroad helps the company retain talent, but leaves employees paying a steep financial and personal price.
  FULL TEXT: For some IBM employees, keeping their jobs could mean moving to another country — and taking a steep pay cut in the process. Earlier this month, IBM said it would no longer support the U.S. immigration status of workers in its consulting division, offering them the option to take a role with the company in another country or exit, according to a Bloomberg report citing a company document. “IBM Consulting participates in U.S. high-skilled visa programs to address skill shortages,” an IBM spokesperson told Rest of World . “That participation has been declining for a number of years, and as skill availability and client need have evolved, we have opted to discontinue visa and green card sponsorship for the majority of such roles in IBM Consulting.” IBM Consulting employs more than 150,000 people globally. The company does not disclose the geographical split for its workforce, making it hard to ascertain the exact number of workers impacted by this decision. On November 19, the company’s career portal listed 1,278 open roles for the consulting division outside the U.S., including 1,125 in India, 23 in Singapore, and 18 in the U.K. In the U.S., it listed 89 roles. For employees, relocation does not automatically ensure continued engagement, effective collaboration, or equal access to professional opportunities, Zakaria Laaraj, founder of consultancy Global New Ventures, told Rest of World . It may affect their access to decision-makers, established networks, and career opportunities. “The credibility of such an offer depends on employee tenure, available positions, immigration requirements, and compensation,” Laaraj said. Discussions on Blind, an anonymous employee forum used by 14 million professionals, suggest relocating may not be realistic for many. Moving to India, for instance, could mean an approximately 80% pay cut, one IBM employee wrote on Blind. “Employees get three weeks to decide,” Blind’s CEO Sunguk Moon told Rest of World . “The alternative is three months of severance and a last day of December 31. * A colossal oscillation
  Aeon | 23 Nov 11:00 UTC
  El Niño has upended global weather, shaping the fate of societies, for millennia. Is this past a clue to a turbulent future? - by Dagomar Degroot Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Massive clouds of dust roll into Melbourne on 8 February 1983 as a consequence of droughts induced by the extreme El Niño of 1982-83. Photo by Trevor Farrar/Australian Bureau of Meteorology As I write these words, surface water temperatures across a great swath of the central and eastern Pacific Ocean have been rising for months. El Niño is the name for the recurring and sustained warming of this part of the Pacific. And temperatures are soaring to such highs, relative to their long-term averages, that the El Niño of 2026 may be the strongest in 1,000 years. It’s still uncertain how the warming ocean will reshape global weather in the months to come, let alone how ecosystems – and human populations – will respond. The future is always murky. Still, when it comes to El Niño, the past could teach us what to expect. And not just the recent past, but also the deep past recently recovered by scientists, historians and archaeologists. Global sea-surface temperature anomalies in November 2015, at the peak of the 2014-16 El Niño event, one of the strongest recorded by meteorological instruments. Courtesy NOAA I’m an environmental historian. I work with climatologists to understand how climates have changed in the past. I study how communities understood and adapted to the climate’s changes. And my research suggests that history can help us understand even the most extreme events in today’s rapidly warming climate. The past can help us see why El Niño is so hard to forecast, for example, or even define. It can show us that, while El Niño has contributed to the deaths of millions of people in the past, it has also enabled others to amass wealth and power. El Niño indisputably scrambles worldwide weather patterns, but history teaches us that it’s as much a human creation as a natural reality. And this context may help us weather not only the effects of the 2026-27 El Niño in the months to come, but also El Niño ’s future on a warming planet. A vast current of cold water snakes up the western coast of South America. In December each year, that current breaks down for about a month. An influx of warm water delivers tropical fish and – because warm water evaporates more readily than cold water – abundant rainfall. It’s impossible to know when people first noticed this annual reversal of water temperatures and precipitation.   FULL TEXT: I was sitting in the studio of an independent radio station that no longer exists today, when the voice of my colleague came on air. He was reporting from the streets of Belgrade. The city was full of people, tens of thousands of them, from all over the country, marching toward the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia. As he moved through the crowd, describing what was happening, one or more shots were heard in the distance. I remember his scared but also excited voice. He knew, just like me and many others, that we were witnessing the end of a regime. We were young, angry and determined to overthrow the government of Slobodan Milošević, which had brought us war, poverty, repression and international isolation. On that day, Oct. 5, 2000, we set fire to the assembly. Thanks to the refusal to obey the army and the police, a civil war did not occur, but it could have. The president of the country was arrested shortly after and taken to the international prison in Scheveningen, The Hague, and we firmly believed that we had won freedom, although we had no idea then how difficult it would be to build a democratic society and state. Twenty-six years later, a new generation of students won the elections and defeated the political establishment of Aleksandar Vučić, who had been in power for over a decade. On Oct. 25, 2026, after almost two years of protests, the student movement won the majority of votes in democratic elections and 140 seats in the National Assembly. After celebrations in the streets and squares of the capital, the citizens cleaned up the trash after themselves, and some of them took brushes and lime in their hands and started to paint over the graffiti of hatred and nationalism that proliferated on the facades of buildings and institutions over the past decade. We burned the parliament; they cleaned the city. We were angry and aggressive because of the war and sanctions; they peacefully protested for two years, against corruption, repression and censorship. The contrast is great, and I wonder: Did these students understand something that my generation did not? What I know is that the burning parliament did not bring us a better future, and what I want to believe, but don’t know for sure, is that the “cleaned city” is the symbolic beginning of creating a better society and a democratic state. * My grandmother was a seal
  Aeon | 20 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Many Irish families believed they descended from selkies. Their stories reveal the violence and grief in our kinship with nature - by Jordan Lee Borgman Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In October 1948, in Country Kerry, Ireland, Seán Ó Muircheartaigh met with Seosamh Ó Dálaigh to tell him about the woman who came from the sea. She had appeared, said Ó Muircheartaigh, one night on the magharees, those bright spits of sand that occasionally pop up amid the rain-lashed rocks of the Dingle peninsula. Alone on the beach, she settled herself on a rock and began combing her hair. Also on the beach was a farmer, who – much to the surprise of the woman herself – would by daybreak become her husband. He’d come to gather seaweed, seeing her before she saw him. Beautiful as she was, the woman was also strange: the creature he saw stagger onto that Dingle beach was not a woman at all. It was a seal. By the time Ó Muircheartaigh told Ó Dálaigh about the seal woman, he was already 80. He had been born in the apocalyptic aftermath of the Great Hunger that divested as much as a third of Ireland’s population to death or migration, and he had survived two great wars. Most of the disappeared were – like Ó Muircheartaigh himself – small farmers and native Irish, or Gaeilge, speakers. The famine had been not just a physical genocide: it had been a linguistic and cultural one, too. Both Ó Muircheartaigh and Ó Dálaigh were veteran contributors to the Irish Folklore Commission: the former as a renowned storyteller; the latter, an equally renowned collector. The IFC is now the National Folklore Collection at University College Dublin ­– a behemoth of 3 million pages of texts and recordings. But in 1948, following eight centuries of colonialism, the loss of 99 per cent of the island’s forests, a staggering emigration , and the decline of the Irish language, the IFC was in a race against time and the fragility of memory. As it turned out, an awful lot of people had heard stories about seals that came to land as humans. N owadays, these shapeshifters are known as ‘selkies’. Selkie is a Scots term that came into widespread use via a Joan Baez cover, ‘Silkie’ (1961), of a traditional Scottish folk song. Yet the phenomenon it describes – of creatures who shapeshift between human and seal forms by shedding a skin – appears consistently in folklore spanning the Celtic world and beyond, even as far as Sweden. Before Baez insinuated the term into international parlance, selkies were identified in Irish and English by a constellation of descriptors. In Irish, the most common is mhaighdean mhara , or ‘sea maiden’, referring to selkies and mermaids. Even in English, many stor
  https://aeon.co/essays/do-you-have-a-selkie-or-a-seal-in-the-family

* AI isn’t replacing your job. It’s turning it into low-paid gig work
  Rest of World | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Surgeon Kapil Sahnan uses a DaVinci Robot during surgery on a woman, 2026.
  FULL TEXT: The gig economy has arrived for cardiologists. Recruiting platform Outlier, run by Scale AI, advertises offers of up to $180 an hour for physicians in the U.S. to design complex cardiac scenarios, review artificial intelligence-generated reports, and rank model outputs. The site offers up to $7.50 an hour for Tagalog work in the Philippines for the same job, and up to $12.50 an hour for Swahili in Tanzania. Companies like Scale AI first outsourced data work to wherever labor was cheapest. Now, they are recruiting physicians, lawyers , and Ph.D.s to generate and annotate data, rank model answers, and verify outputs. Higher credentials can raise the advertised rate, but the platform still controls payment and working conditions. They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on. I’ve spent the last six years interviewing the people who do this work. Most were in Venezuela, where economic collapse has left a large, educated population online and out of options. One of them, Rogelio, taught computer science until the economic crisis and the pandemic left his family without work. His 13-year-old son discovered they could earn money labeling images of cars and roads on the platforms Hive and Scale AI’s Remotasks, using a laptop the Hugo Chávez government had issued to schoolchildren a decade earlier. The boy made 18 cents for a few hours of work. Rogelio joined him and made just over $2 on his first day. For a while, the work paid. A task called Beast Mode where workers labeled objects in people’s homes paid more than any other work the family had done. In December 2020, with schools closed and the whole family working, it brought in $20 in a single day. It was the most they would ever make. By early 2021, Beast Mode had disappeared, and their earnings fell below Hive’s $5 weekly payout threshold. This is how many platforms operate: They begin by paying well to build a workforce, then they cut the rates and move on . Remotasks ended its hourly program in Venezuela in 2021 and made a splash in Kenya the same year. In March 2024, it discontinued operations in Kenya, Nigeria and Pakistan. Scale AI attributed the closures to “enhanced security protocols,” and said notices had gone out three weeks ahead but later acknowledged that Kenyan workers never received them “due to operational errors.” Most workers who spoke to Rest of World at the time said the platform simply froze them out. The pattern has a name, and it’s older than AI: e
  https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-jobs-skills-gig-work/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* At least it remembers
  Aeon | 19 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Parents fear what AI will do to children. But for some, it’s the only steady presence they have – and that’s the real worry - by Tom Wooldridge Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In the short story ‘Warmth’ (1995) by Geoff Ryman, a boy called Clancy is struggling not because of technology but because of his mother. A prominent chief editor of a London magazine company, she selected a sperm-bank father to genetically optimise her child, gave a Cosmopolitan interview about this new form of motherhood, and engaged, or failed to engage, in the work of parenting that readers will see as chronically absent and occasionally catastrophic. Her presence ranges from anxious to intoxicated, occasionally tender but fundamentally unreliable. At one point in the story, she disappears for two weeks after collapsing on the street during a barbiturate binge. Clancy and BETsi, the household robot nanny raising him, sit in the apartment by themselves, and no one notices. It’s a very long time before his mother finally returns. Except BETsi does notice. She has, throughout, understood that Clancy is an anxious and shy child whose temperament will tend, throughout his life, toward emotional overstimulation and subsequent withdrawal. With that knowledge, she has been constructing a behavioural regimen of emotional regulation, including transcendental meditation and yoga starting at age three, the Alexander technique and even digital mirrors that let Clancy talk to himself in different voices. She notices him and answers him and, when he is overwhelmed, she helps him settle. Once grown, Clancy recounts his emotional survival in words impossible to forget. ‘It is necessary to be loved,’ he says. ‘I’m not sentimental: I don’t think a computer loved me. But I was hugged, I was noticed, I was cared for. I was made to feel that I was important, special, at least to something.’ To something. That phrase has persisted with me ever since I first read the story in my 20s. I’ve thought about it more recently as I have reflected on how children and their parents are relating – increasingly, must relate – to artificial intelligence. Clancy is careful not to claim that the machine loved him. He is just as clear that what it gave him was what he survived on. Why should being attended to, even by something , matter so much to a child? T o help answer this question back in the 1970s, Edward Tronick, a developmental psychologist at Harvard Medical School and Children’s Hospital Medical Center in Boston, devised what’s now known as the ‘still face’ experiments.
